Through a systematic layer-wise analysis of pre-trained diffusion models (DiT and U-Net based), the authors observe that self-attention predominantly exhibits localized attention patterns, resembling convolutional inductive biases.  Based on this observation, they propose ConvFusion, replacing self-attention modules with Pyramid Convolution Blocks (△ConvBlocks). These blocks are designed to mimic the two key components of observed self-attention: a high-frequency distance-dependent signal and a low-frequency, spatially invariant bias. They achieve comparable or superior image generation quality while significantly reducing computational cost. They use knowledge distillation during training to efficiently transfer the knowledge from the original self-attention model to the convolution-based model. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of ConvFusion in both U-Net and DiT architectures, demonstrating strong performance and versatility while significantly reducing computational demands.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing self-attention with convolutions in diffusion models, while not entirely new, is executed with impressive analysis and a carefully designed architecture.  The detailed analysis of attention maps, breaking down self-attention into frequency components and showing its localized nature, is a strong contribution. The specific design of the AConvBlocks to capture these characteristics is also novel.  The paper doesn't simply replace self-attention; it attempts to *distill* its learned patterns into a more efficient convolutional representation. The use of knowledge distillation to transfer the learned patterns efficiently is a key practical aspect of the work.

*   **Significance:**  The quadratic computational complexity of self-attention is a significant bottleneck in diffusion models, especially for high-resolution image generation.  The reported 6929x reduction in computational cost is a substantial achievement and could have a significant impact on the practical application of diffusion models.  The fact that the approach works for both DiT and U-Net architectures further increases its significance. While previous works have attempted to mitigate the cost of self-attention, ConvFusion offers a more radical approach by effectively eliminating it entirely. Demonstrating that global interaction may not be as critical in diffusion models as previously assumed opens up new avenues for exploration. The superior performance compared to LinFusion suggests its significant contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides compelling quantitative results (FLOPs reduction, inference speedup, and image quality metrics).
    *   **Clear Analysis:** The attention map analysis and decomposition are well-executed and provide a solid justification for the proposed approach.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:** The AConvBlocks are designed in a principled manner, directly addressing the observed characteristics of self-attention.
    *   **Efficient Training:** The use of knowledge distillation and freezing most parameters during training is a practical and effective strategy.
    *   **Orthogonal to existing speed-ups:** The combination with AST indicates the potential for further improvements.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on Pre-trained Models:** The approach relies on distilling knowledge from pre-trained models. While effective in practice, it's important to understand the limitations. How well would ConvFusion perform if trained from scratch? Would the same locality properties of attention emerge? The transfer of knowledge might influence the observed locality.
    *   **Lack of exploration on larger models:** Testing the scalability of their method in larger models or more complex datasets. The current experiments are limited to SD1.5, SDXL and PixArt.
    *   **Limited ablation studies:** The ablations should be more detailed regarding the contributions of pyramid and average pooling components.
    *   **Limited comparison to other fast diffusion method**: There are recent alternatives like structured state space models that exhibit high speedup and memory efficiency, so the current comparison is not exhaustive.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could shift the focus of research towards more efficient, convolution-based architectures for diffusion models. It may inspire new techniques for distilling knowledge from existing self-attention models and combining these with efficient, structured convolution designs. The paper opens the door for a new generation of faster, more accessible diffusion models.

**Score: 8.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of diffusion models by challenging the assumed necessity of self-attention and providing a compelling alternative with substantial computational benefits. The well-supported analysis of self-attention, the principled design of the convolutional architecture, and the strong empirical results justify a high score. However, the dependence on pre-trained models, the incomplete exploration of various existing baseline model and limited ablation studies and the potential dependence of the method on properties inherent to attention during training prevent it from achieving a higher score. Nonetheless, its potential impact on making diffusion models more practical and accessible earns it a score above 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Fairness in Graph Learning Augmented with Machine Learning: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21296v1)
- **Authors**: Renqiang Luo, Ziqi Xu, Xikun Zhang, Qing Qing, Huafei Huang, Enyan Dai, Zhe Wang, Bo Yang
- **Abstract**: Augmenting specialised machine learning techniques into traditional graph learning models has achieved notable success across various domains, including federated graph learning, dynamic graph learning, and graph transformers. However, the intricate mechanisms of these specialised techniques introduce significant challenges in maintaining model fairness, potentially resulting in discriminatory outcomes in high-stakes applications such as recommendation systems, disaster response, criminal justice, and loan approval. This paper systematically examines the unique fairness challenges posed by Graph Learning augmented with Machine Learning (GL-ML). It highlights the complex interplay between graph learning mechanisms and machine learning techniques, emphasising how the augmentation of machine learning both enhances and complicates fairness. Additionally, we explore four critical techniques frequently employed to improve fairness in GL-ML methods. By thoroughly investigating the root causes and broader implications of fairness challenges in this rapidly evolving field, this work establishes a robust foundation for future research and innovation in GL-ML fairness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Fairness in Graph Learning Augmented with Machine Learning: A Survey" addresses the emerging challenge of fairness in graph learning (GL) models that are enhanced with machine learning (ML) techniques (GL-ML). While GL-ML methods have shown great promise in various domains, the integration of ML can introduce new sources of bias and discrimination. The survey explores the unique fairness challenges in GL-ML, highlighting the interplay between graph learning mechanisms and ML techniques, and how this augmentation can complicate fairness. It then categorizes existing works into traditional fairness-aware graph learning and GL-ML and explores four critical techniques used to improve fairness in GL-ML.  The paper seeks to provide a foundation for future research and innovation in fairness within the GL-ML framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's main strength is its focus on a relatively unexplored area: the fairness challenges arising specifically from *augmenting* graph learning with machine learning. While fairness in graph learning has been studied, the nuanced issues and new biases introduced *by* incorporating different ML techniques (federated learning, transformers, condensation, etc.) are a less-trodden path. The establishment of GL-ML as a distinct fairness domain is novel.  However, the individual ML techniques mentioned are each independently well-studied in terms of fairness, which dilutes the overall novelty somewhat; a deeper dive into how their *combination* with graph methods creates novel, unpredicted challenges would further strengthen the argument.

* **Significance:**  The paper tackles a highly relevant and increasingly important problem. As GL-ML models are deployed in high-stakes applications (recommendation, disaster response, criminal justice, loan approval), the consequences of unfair outcomes become significant. By systematically examining these challenges and categorizing existing methods, the survey provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working in this area.  The paper also clearly identifies open challenges, setting a roadmap for future research directions. The identification of novel fairness metrics and the need for privacy-preserving fairness solutions are particularly significant. The categorization of approaches, while somewhat broad, is still useful for navigating the literature.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of fairness in GL-ML and explains why traditional fairness metrics may not be sufficient.
    * **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a broad range of relevant techniques, including federated learning, graph transformers, condensation methods, etc.
    * **Identifies Open Challenges:** The paper highlights several important open challenges, such as the need for novel metrics, privacy-preserving fairness solutions, and targeted fairness-aware methods.
    * **Well-structured and Organized:** The paper is well-structured, making it easy to follow and understand the key concepts.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Depth of Analysis:** While the coverage is comprehensive, the depth of analysis for each technique could be improved. More detailed case studies or comparative analyses would strengthen the arguments.  Specifically, while the paper mentions, for example, the problem of attention bias in Graph Transformers, it doesn't deeply analyze specific mechanisms for mitigating it.
    * **Limited Empirical Validation:**  As a survey, the paper doesn't include new empirical evaluations. However, discussing the performance of existing methods (in terms of fairness and utility) would be beneficial.
    * **Categorization Limitations:** The taxonomy, while helpful, could be more fine-grained. The "Via Machine Learning" category is quite broad. More specific sub-categories reflecting the type of ML technique used would be useful.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by raising awareness of the fairness challenges in GL-ML and by providing a roadmap for future research. It could also inspire the development of new fairness-aware methods that are specifically designed for GL-ML applications. However, its impact will depend on whether it can spur more targeted research into the specifically *combinatorial* fairness challenges of GL-ML rather than viewing it as an aggregate of the fairness issues inherent in each component independently.

**Overall:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of fairness in machine learning, specifically within the context of graph learning augmented with ML techniques. It addresses a timely and important problem and provides a useful framework for future research. While the depth of analysis could be improved, the paper's clear problem definition, comprehensive coverage, and identification of open challenges make it a significant contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score of 7.5 reflects the paper's strong relevance and clear identification of an important emerging research area. The novelty, while present, is tempered by the fact that much of the content draws from existing, well-trodden fairness research on the individual machine learning components. The paper would benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the interactions between graph learning and machine learning, and how these interactions can create unique fairness challenges. Also, the categorization of methods, though useful, has room for improvement by being more specific to types of ML techniques used, which would result in a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### BiasGuard: A Reasoning-enhanced Bias Detection Tool For Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21299v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiting Fan, Ruizhe Chen, Zuozhu Liu
- **Abstract**: Identifying bias in LLM-generated content is a crucial prerequisite for ensuring fairness in LLMs. Existing methods, such as fairness classifiers and LLM-based judges, face limitations related to difficulties in understanding underlying intentions and the lack of criteria for fairness judgment. In this paper, we introduce BiasGuard, a novel bias detection tool that explicitly analyzes inputs and reasons through fairness specifications to provide accurate judgments. BiasGuard is implemented through a two-stage approach: the first stage initializes the model to explicitly reason based on fairness specifications, while the second stage leverages reinforcement learning to enhance its reasoning and judgment capabilities. Our experiments, conducted across five datasets, demonstrate that BiasGuard outperforms existing tools, improving accuracy and reducing over-fairness misjudgments. We also highlight the importance of reasoning-enhanced decision-making and provide evidence for the effectiveness of our two-stage optimization pipeline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the BiasGuard paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BiasGuard, a novel tool for detecting bias in LLM-generated text. BiasGuard addresses the limitations of existing methods (fairness classifiers and LLM-based judges) by explicitly analyzing inputs and reasoning through fairness specifications. It employs a two-stage approach: the first stage initializes the model to reason based on predefined fairness criteria, while the second stage leverages reinforcement learning (RL) to improve reasoning and judgment. Experiments across five datasets demonstrate that BiasGuard outperforms existing tools in accuracy and in reducing over-fairness misjudgments. The authors highlight the importance of reasoning-enhanced decision-making and provide evidence for the effectiveness of their two-stage optimization pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Explicit Reasoning:** The core novelty lies in incorporating explicit reasoning into the bias detection process. This addresses a significant limitation of existing classifiers, which often struggle with implicit biases due to their reliance on surface-level patterns. The use of fairness specifications provides a structured approach to evaluating text, moving beyond simple classification.
    *   **Two-Stage Optimization:** The two-stage approach of SFT followed by RL is a well-established strategy for training LLMs, offering a balance between imitating desired behavior and exploring the solution space.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation across five datasets, including those containing both explicit and implicit biases. This provides strong evidence for BiasGuard's effectiveness and generalizability. The comparison with existing tools, including both classifiers and LLMs-as-judges, demonstrates a clear improvement.
    *   **Reduction of Over-Fairness:**  The paper explicitly mentions reducing "over-fairness," a common issue where models incorrectly label biased content as fair. This is a relevant contribution as it highlights a practical benefit of the approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies effectively highlight the importance of reasoning capabilities and the importance of each stage within the overall pipeline.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Verifiability of Reasoning:** As the authors themselves acknowledge, the reasoning process within BiasGuard is not directly verifiable. This creates a "black box" aspect, making it difficult to fully understand why the model makes certain judgments. This is a valid point in the paper that provides some context for future works.
    *   **Reliance on Teacher Model:** The initial stage relies on a teacher model to generate reasoning trajectories. This introduces a potential dependency on the teacher model's biases and limitations. Although this is still a very innovative approach, it must be noted that this is one area where the authors can make improvements.
    *   **Limited Novelty in RL Usage:** While the RL component is well-integrated, the overall RL framework doesn't present any radical departures from existing techniques.

*   **Significance:**

    *   BiasGuard represents a meaningful advance in automated bias detection. The explicit reasoning component addresses a critical shortcoming of current methods, leading to improved accuracy and robustness. The tool has the potential to contribute to the development of fairer and more responsible LLMs.
    *   The emphasis on fairness specifications encourages a more structured and transparent approach to bias detection, which could influence future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

BiasGuard makes a significant contribution by integrating explicit reasoning into automated bias detection. The empirical results support the effectiveness of the approach, demonstrating improvements over existing methods. The reduction of over-fairness is also a valuable feature.

However, limitations remain regarding the verifiability of the reasoning process and the reliance on a teacher model. While the RL component contributes to performance, it doesn't introduce any radical innovations.

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is justified. The paper presents a novel and effective approach to bias detection, but also identifies areas for future improvement.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Confidence in Large Language Model Evaluation: A Bayesian Approach to Limited-Sample Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21303v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Xiao, Yu Su, Sijing Zhang, Zhang Chen, Yadong Chen, Tian Liu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit probabilistic output characteristics, yet conventional evaluation frameworks rely on deterministic scalar metrics. This study introduces a Bayesian approach for LLM capability assessment that integrates prior knowledge through probabilistic inference, addressing limitations under limited-sample regimes. By treating model capabilities as latent variables and leveraging a curated query set to induce discriminative responses, we formalize model ranking as a Bayesian hypothesis testing problem over mutually exclusive capability intervals. Experimental evaluations with GPT-series models demonstrate that the proposed method achieves superior discrimination compared to conventional evaluation methods. Results indicate that even with reduced sample sizes, the approach maintains statistical robustness while providing actionable insights, such as probabilistic statements about a model's likelihood of surpassing specific baselines. This work advances LLM evaluation methodologies by bridging Bayesian inference with practical constraints in real-world deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Bayesian approach to evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly addressing the challenges posed by limited sample sizes. It proposes treating LLM capabilities as latent variables and using a curated query set to induce discriminative responses. The method formulates model ranking as a Bayesian hypothesis testing problem across mutually exclusive capability intervals. The paper demonstrates that this Bayesian approach provides superior discrimination and statistical robustness compared to conventional evaluation methods, even with reduced sample sizes, offering probabilistic statements about model performance. Experiments with GPT series models validate the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper's central novelty lies in its adoption and adaptation of Bayesian inference principles to the specific problem of LLM evaluation under limited data scenarios. While Bayesian methods are not entirely new to model evaluation, their systematic application to LLM capability ranking, coupled with a focus on practical constraints and prior knowledge integration (especially response distribution patterns across a curated dataset), provides a unique contribution. The approach directly addresses a significant limitation of traditional scalar-based metrics: their inability to reliably discern model performance differences when sample sizes are small. The paper also highlights the importance of *prior knowledge*, which can be incorporated from the response distribution patterns of other LLMs across a curated dataset.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Limited sample sizes are a common constraint in real-world LLM evaluation, making the paper's focus highly practical.
*   **Novel application of Bayesian inference:** Adapting Bayesian principles for LLM ranking, considering mutually exclusive capability intervals, is a meaningful step.
*   **Emphasis on practical constraints:** The method attempts to bridge the gap between theoretical Bayesian inference and real-world deployment scenarios. The use of curated datasets to provide prior information is practical.
*   **Empirical validation:** Experiments comparing the Bayesian approach to common evaluation methods (accuracy, Pass@N, mean/std) provide evidence of its superiority.  The robustness analysis by reducing query set sizes strengthens the paper's claim.
*   **Clear explanation:** The paper generally explains the methodology in a comprehensive and understandable manner.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Query independence assumption:**  The assumption of conditional query independence (Eq. 3) could be a significant limitation. Semantically correlated queries could violate this assumption and potentially skew results. The paper acknowledges this limitation, but the impact could be more thoroughly explored. Modeling question interdependencies should be addressed more directly.
*   **Dependence on Anchor Model Selection:** The method relies heavily on the quality and distribution of "anchor" models. If the capabilities of these anchor models are poorly defined or have large uncertainties themselves, it could propagate errors into the evaluation. The paper discusses automated anchor selection which would be critical to robust application.
*   **Limited Scope of Validation:** The validation is primarily focused on GPT series models. Further testing across more diverse LLM architectures and tasks would strengthen the generalization claims.  Also, the choice of comparing to relatively basic metrics (accuracy, Pass@k, mean/std) may not fully demonstrate superiority against other more sophisticated probabilistic methods in education research (Rasch models), or Elo rating systems. While the paper mentions these alternatives, a direct comparison would be useful.
*   **Prior Knowledge Acquired Empirically:** The 'prior knowledge' refers to data gathered from diverse LLMs on question datasets. It requires substantial work to curate quality question sets.
*   **Binary scoring paradigm:** The binary scoring system is suitable for LLMs performing well with well-defined tasks.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the development of more robust and reliable LLM evaluation methodologies. Its focus on Bayesian inference and prior knowledge integration provides a valuable framework for addressing the limitations of traditional evaluation metrics, especially in data-constrained scenarios. The work could influence the design of future LLM evaluation benchmarks and tools, promoting a more nuanced understanding of model capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution to LLM evaluation, some limitations, particularly the query independence assumption and reliance on good anchor models, hold it back from being truly exceptional. The empirical validation, while promising, also needs to be expanded across more diverse LLMs and comparison to other probabilistic evaluation models. A score of 7 reflects the paper's significance, novelty, and practical relevance, while acknowledging the areas that require further investigation and refinement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Dual Power of Interpretable Token Embeddings: Jailbreaking Attacks and Defenses for Diffusion Model Unlearning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21307v1)
- **Authors**: Siyi Chen, Yimeng Zhang, Sijia Liu, Qing Qu
- **Abstract**: Despite the remarkable generalization capabilities of diffusion models, recent studies have shown that these models can memorize and generate harmful content when prompted with specific text instructions. Although fine-tuning approaches have been developed to mitigate this issue by unlearning harmful concepts, these methods can be easily circumvented through jailbreaking attacks. This indicates that the harmful concept has not been fully erased from the model. However, existing attack methods, while effective, lack interpretability regarding why unlearned models still retain the concept, thereby hindering the development of defense strategies. In this work, we address these limitations by proposing an attack method that learns an orthogonal set of interpretable attack token embeddings. The attack token embeddings can be decomposed into human-interpretable textual elements, revealing that unlearned models still retain the target concept through implicit textual components. Furthermore, these attack token embeddings are robust and transferable across text prompts, initial noises, and unlearned models. Finally, leveraging this diverse set of embeddings, we design a defense method applicable to both our proposed attack and existing attack methods. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of both our attack and defense strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores jailbreaking attacks and defenses for diffusion models that have undergone unlearning, particularly focusing on the stable diffusion model. It introduces a novel attack method called SubAttack, which learns a set of interpretable, orthogonal token embeddings to regenerate harmful content, even in models designed to mitigate such outputs.  SubAttack decomposes these embeddings into human-interpretable textual elements, revealing implicit textual components that retain the harmful concept. The authors demonstrate the robustness and transferability of SubAttack across different prompts, noises, and unlearned models. Building on these findings, they propose a defense mechanism, SubDefense, that removes the learned attack token embeddings via orthogonal subspace projection. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of both the attack and defense strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its emphasis on *interpretability* in attacking unlearned diffusion models. While prior work has explored jailbreaking attacks, this paper aims to understand *why* unlearned models remain vulnerable by analyzing the learned attack token embeddings. The concept of learning an *orthogonal set* of attack tokens and decomposing them into interpretable CLIP token combinations to expose the weaknesses of unlearning methods is reasonably novel. Furthermore, the SubDefense method is novel, providing a technique to mitigate various attacks by removing learnt attack token embeddings via orthogonal subspace projection.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several aspects:
    *   It reveals limitations of current unlearning techniques by demonstrating that they often fail to completely remove harmful concepts, leaving models vulnerable to attacks exploiting implicit textual associations.
    *   The proposed interpretable attack allows for a deeper understanding of the vulnerabilities, which is crucial for developing more robust defenses.
    *   The proposed defense method provides a practical approach to enhance the safety of unlearned diffusion models.
    *   The work highlights the need for future unlearning research to focus on removing implicit concept associations within pre-trained models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed methods and experimental setup.
    *   The interpretability analysis provides valuable insights into the inner workings of diffusion models and the challenges of unlearning.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of both the attack and defense strategies, with comparisons to existing methods.
    *   The experiments appear fairly comprehensive, exploring robustness, transferability, and interpretability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the interpretability analysis is valuable, the "bag-of-words" approach might oversimplify the complex relationships between concepts in the model.
    *   The reliance on a specific architecture (Stable Diffusion v1.4) limits the generalizability of the findings, although the underlying concepts likely apply more broadly.
    *   The orthogonal subspace projection is straightforward, so while the motivation is strong, the defense mechanism itself may not be exceptionally technically sophisticated. It is not clear if the defence is optimal and what implications does the protection have on downstream performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in diffusion model security and unlearning. By highlighting the importance of implicit concept associations, it points the direction for developing more effective unlearning algorithms.  The proposed defense mechanism can serve as a baseline for future defense strategies. The emphasis on interpretability will likely inspire further research aimed at understanding and controlling the behavior of diffusion models.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid contribution with significant novelty and practical implications.  The interpretability aspect is a key strength that distinguishes it from prior works in the jailbreaking of diffusion models. The defense mechanism is a good contribution and is simple yet effective. However, the limitations regarding the specific architecture, potential oversimplification in the interpretability analysis, and relatively straightforward defense mechanism slightly reduce the overall impact, preventing a higher score. Nevertheless, the work is valuable and contributes meaningfully to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Evaluation of a Visual Question Answering Strategy for Zero-shot Facial Expression Recognition in Still Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21309v1)
- **Authors**: Modesto Castrillón-Santana, Oliverio J Santana, David Freire-Obregón, Daniel Hernández-Sosa, Javier Lorenzo-Navarro
- **Abstract**: Facial expression recognition (FER) is a key research area in computer vision and human-computer interaction. Despite recent advances in deep learning, challenges persist, especially in generalizing to new scenarios. In fact, zero-shot FER significantly reduces the performance of state-of-the-art FER models. To address this problem, the community has recently started to explore the integration of knowledge from Large Language Models for visual tasks. In this work, we evaluate a broad collection of locally executed Visual Language Models (VLMs), avoiding the lack of task-specific knowledge by adopting a Visual Question Answering strategy. We compare the proposed pipeline with state-of-the-art FER models, both integrating and excluding VLMs, evaluating well-known FER benchmarks: AffectNet, FERPlus, and RAF-DB. The results show excellent performance for some VLMs in zero-shot FER scenarios, indicating the need for further exploration to improve FER generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Visual Language Models (VLMs) for zero-shot Facial Expression Recognition (FER).  Instead of task-specific training, the authors adopt a Visual Question Answering (VQA) strategy, prompting VLMs with different questions related to the facial expression in an image.  They evaluate a variety of locally executed VLMs on standard FER benchmarks (AffectNet, FERPlus, and RAF-DB) and compare their performance with state-of-the-art FER models, both with and without VLM integration. The study reveals that some VLMs exhibit excellent performance in zero-shot FER scenarios, highlighting the need for further research to improve FER generalization using VLMs. Prompt engineering is found to be crucial in optimizing VLM performance for the FER task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic evaluation of various VLMs within a VQA framework specifically for zero-shot FER *without* task-specific fine-tuning or training. While using VLMs for visual tasks and even FER isn't entirely new, the comprehensive comparison of different models and prompts on standard benchmarks and the focus on true zero-shot transfer, makes it distinct. The lack of focus on task-specific training is noteworthy, contrasting with approaches that adapt VLMs through fine-tuning or contrastive learning. The attempt to map potentially open-ended answers from the LLM-based VQA to the restricted categories (anger, disgust, etc.) through synonym mapping is another aspect of innovation. However, the prompt engineering method itself is not novel, but the effort to establish VQA and LLM-answer mapping for FER constitutes incremental innovation.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper lies in providing a practical demonstration of leveraging powerful pre-trained VLMs for a specialized computer vision task like FER *without* the need for extensive task-specific data. The results show that certain VLMs can achieve competitive, and sometimes even better, performance than models trained on specific FER datasets, demonstrating the potential of knowledge transfer from broad visual and linguistic knowledge. This is especially important given the generalization limitations of traditional FER models.  The study also highlights the importance of prompt engineering in guiding VLMs and extracting relevant information for the task, suggesting avenues for future research to optimize prompt design. The VQA based prompt-engineering approach seems promising in a zero-shot setting. The detailed evaluation across different VLMs and prompts is a valuable resource for the community. One weakness is the dependency on ChatGPT for answer mapping.  This introduces another, somewhat arbitrary, element into the process.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a relatively broad range of VLMs (BLIP-2, Florence-VL, LLAMA 3.2, PaliGemma) using a consistent methodology.
*   **Focus on True Zero-Shot:** The emphasis on zero-shot performance without any task-specific training data is crucial for evaluating the generalization capabilities of the VLMs.
*   **Prompt Engineering Investigation:** The systematic exploration of different prompts offers insights into how to effectively elicit relevant information from VLMs.
*   **Benchmark Datasets:** Using standard datasets for evaluation allows for comparison with existing FER models.
*   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is generally well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and discussion.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Synonym Mapping:** The reliance on an external LLM (ChatGPT) to map diverse answers to the standard FER categories introduces an element of subjectivity and potential bias. The quality and coverage of the synonyms are critical.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the confusion matrices provide some insights, a more detailed analysis of specific cases where the VLMs fail would be beneficial to understand the limitations.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not adequately address the computational cost associated with running large VLMs, which is a significant practical consideration.
*   **Lack of Novelty on Prompt Engineering:** The prompt engineering method itself is not very original and may benefit from exploring more sophisticated prompting techniques.
*   **The approach has limitations:** The paper points out that LLMs sometimes have answers not included in the basic facial expression mappings or the answer is not coherent with the person emotion. This can affect the quality of the results.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in FER by:

*   Encouraging the adoption of VLMs for zero-shot FER and other related tasks.
*   Motivating further exploration of prompt engineering techniques for VLMs.
*   Highlighting the importance of evaluating generalization capabilities.
*   Providing a valuable benchmark for comparing different VLM-based FER approaches.

**Justification for the score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by exploring the use of VLMs for zero-shot FER. While the individual components of the approach are not entirely novel, the *systematic evaluation*, and the demonstration of reasonably good zero-shot FER without specific training data is valuable. The limitations in synonym mapping and the dependence on a separate service (ChatGPT) as well as the relatively small contribution regarding novel prompt design limit the score. In addition, there is a dependency on basic or general VLMs that might provide lower performance in FER than specifically designed FER systems. However, the paper provides an excellent starting point for future work in this area.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Capturing Conditional Dependence via Auto-regressive Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21314v1)
- **Authors**: Xunpeng Huang, Yujin Han, Difan Zou, Yian Ma, Tong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated appealing performance in both image and video generation. However, many works discover that they struggle to capture important, high-level relationships that are present in the real world. For example, they fail to learn physical laws from data, and even fail to understand that the objects in the world exist in a stable fashion. This is due to the fact that important conditional dependence structures are not adequately captured in the vanilla diffusion models. In this work, we initiate an in-depth study on strengthening the diffusion model to capture the conditional dependence structures in the data. In particular, we examine the efficacy of the auto-regressive (AR) diffusion models for such purpose and develop the first theoretical results on the sampling error of AR diffusion models under (possibly) the mildest data assumption. Our theoretical findings indicate that, compared with typical diffusion models, the AR variant produces samples with a reduced gap in approximating the data conditional distribution. On the other hand, the overall inference time of the AR-diffusion models is only moderately larger than that for the vanilla diffusion models, making them still practical for large scale applications. We also provide empirical results showing that when there is clear conditional dependence structure in the data, the AR diffusion models captures such structure, whereas vanilla DDPM fails to do so. On the other hand, when there is no obvious conditional dependence across patches of the data, AR diffusion does not outperform DDPM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an in-depth study on capturing conditional dependencies using auto-regressive (AR) diffusion models. It theoretically analyzes the sampling error of AR diffusion models, demonstrating they can better approximate data conditional distributions compared to standard diffusion models with comparable inference times. Empirical results on synthetic datasets corroborate the theory, showing AR diffusion captures conditional dependence structures effectively when they exist, unlike vanilla DDPM. However, it observes that AR diffusion does not outperform DDPM when such dependencies are absent.  The paper also investigates the impact of score estimation errors on the comparison of inference performance between AR and standard diffusion models, both theoretically and experimentally.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in providing a **first rigorous theoretical analysis** of AR diffusion models. While AR diffusion models themselves aren't entirely new, prior work focused more on practical scalability. The paper's theoretical treatment of their conditional dependence capturing capabilities is a significant contribution. Additionally, the analysis comparing score estimation errors between AR and non-AR models is valuable. Providing conditions necessary for AR diffusion models to attain convergence is also innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to be quite significant. Diffusion models are increasingly important for image, video, and audio generation, so any advance in their ability to capture underlying structure is valuable. The work provides:
    *   **Theoretical justification** for using AR diffusion when conditional dependencies are present.
    *   **Practical guidance** for understanding the trade-offs (slightly increased inference time vs. better conditional approximation).
    *   **A framework** for analyzing the impact of score estimation errors on performance.
    *   **Direction for further research** by highlighting the conditions under which AR diffusion is most effective and areas for improvement.
    *   Experiment on real-world datastes provide supporting facts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous theoretical analysis:** The paper derives important theoretical results on the convergence and efficiency of AR diffusion models.
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of vanilla diffusion models in capturing conditional dependencies.
    *   **Strong empirical validation:**  Experiments on synthetic datasets strongly support the theoretical findings.
    *   **Comprehensive comparison:** A detailed comparison to standard diffusion models is provided.
    *   **Well-written and well-organized**: It is also overall a well-structured paper that provides clear intuition behind its claims.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited empirical scope:** While the synthetic datasets are helpful for validating the theory, experiments on more complex, real-world datasets would strengthen the claims. The connection to the "real world" is primarily through abstractions such as light/shadow and arithmetic sequences in numbers.
    *   **Incremental improvement:** AR diffusion is not a paradigm shift, the improvements shown are still relatively limited. While this can be attributed to the synthetic nature of the datasets, further demonstrations on real data could emphasize a more significant impact.
    *   **Complexity:** The theoretical analysis is quite complex, which might make the paper less accessible to practitioners. A simpler, more intuitive explanation of the key theoretical insights would broaden its impact.
    *   **Over claiming:** The paper claims to provide the most mild assumptions for convergence. While the claim is true for the conditional dependence scenario the analysis doesn't discuss if the results hold as the standard diffusion models for other datasets with similar assumptions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a strong foundation for future research on AR diffusion models. It could inspire:
    *   Development of new AR diffusion architectures tailored to specific conditional dependence structures.
    *   Exploration of techniques to reduce the inference time overhead of AR diffusion.
    *   Application of AR diffusion to a wider range of generative modeling tasks.
    * Improvement to previous work.

*   **Justification:** The paper makes a substantial contribution to the theoretical understanding of AR diffusion models and provides practical insights for their use. It bridges the gap between theory and practice and serves as an invaluable reference to upcoming researchers on diffusion models.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Text-Conditioned Diffusion Model for High-Fidelity Korean Font Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21325v1)
- **Authors**: Abdul Sami, Avinash Kumar, Irfanullah Memon, Youngwon Jo, Muhammad Rizwan, Jaeyoung Choi
- **Abstract**: Automatic font generation (AFG) is the process of creating a new font using only a few examples of the style images. Generating fonts for complex languages like Korean and Chinese, particularly in handwritten styles, presents significant challenges. Traditional AFGs, like Generative adversarial networks (GANs) and Variational Auto-Encoders (VAEs), are usually unstable during training and often face mode collapse problems. They also struggle to capture fine details within font images. To address these problems, we present a diffusion-based AFG method which generates high-quality, diverse Korean font images using only a single reference image, focusing on handwritten and printed styles. Our approach refines noisy images incrementally, ensuring stable training and visually appealing results. A key innovation is our text encoder, which processes phonetic representations to generate accurate and contextually correct characters, even for unseen characters. We used a pre-trained style encoder from DG FONT to effectively and accurately encode the style images. To further enhance the generation quality, we used perceptual loss that guides the model to focus on the global style of generated images. Experimental results on over 2000 Korean characters demonstrate that our model consistently generates accurate and detailed font images and outperforms benchmark methods, making it a reliable tool for generating authentic Korean fonts across different styles.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DK-Font, a novel diffusion model for high-fidelity Korean font generation, particularly focusing on handwritten and complex styles. DK-Font builds upon existing diffusion models by incorporating several key improvements: a text encoder to process phonetic representations for better content understanding, an enhanced character attribute encoder combining text embeddings with content, style, and stroke information, and the use of perceptual loss during training to improve visual quality and detail. The model is trained on a large Korean character dataset and evaluated against a baseline (Diff-Font) using metrics like SSIM, RMSE, LPIPS, and FID. The results demonstrate that DK-Font consistently generates more accurate, detailed, and stylistically consistent Korean fonts compared to the baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of Korean font generation, particularly for complex handwritten styles, and acknowledges the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Methodological Novelty:** The integration of a text encoder with a diffusion model is a valuable contribution. The addition of enhanced character attributes encoder and perceptual loss further strengthens the model's capabilities. These components collectively address key issues like content loss, style inconsistency, and component misplacement, which are common in previous font generation methods.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The quantitative results consistently demonstrate DK-Font's superiority over the baseline. The visual comparisons also support these findings, showcasing improved detail and stylistic consistency.
    *   **Focus on a Challenging Language:** The focus on Korean, a language with complex character structures, adds to the significance of the work.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The use of a curated, robust Korean font dataset contributes to the reliability and generalizability of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Comparison to State-of-the-Art:** The paper primarily compares against Diff-Font. While it acknowledges other related works, a more direct comparison to other state-of-the-art few-shot or one-shot font generation methods (especially those targeting complex languages) would strengthen the evaluation.  A more thorough literature review of other methods addressing similar issues and datasets would make the contributions more distinct.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** While the improvements (text encoder, perceptual loss) are justified, ablation studies to quantify the individual impact of each component on performance would strengthen the paper.
    *   **No User Study:** The paper relies primarily on quantitative metrics.  A user study to assess the perceived quality and realism of the generated fonts would provide valuable complementary evidence.
    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size, while substantial, is not huge for diffusion models, especially for character generation. The impact of scaling the dataset would be a useful discussion point.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** The paper lacks a discussion of the computational cost associated with training and inference, which is an important consideration for practical applications.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The paper presents a useful combination of existing techniques to tackle a specific problem with demonstrable improvements in Korean font generation. The key novelty lies in effectively adapting and combining existing components within a diffusion framework, addressing key challenges specific to font generation for complex languages, but the novelty is somewhat incremental. It is not a fundamentally new algorithm or framework. The significance lies in addressing a practical problem with tangible results. While the approach is effective, a greater level of comparative analysis with other methods is needed to further strengthen its position.

**Overall Assessment:**

DK-Font presents a solid contribution to the field of font generation, with a particular focus on Korean language.  The integration of a text encoder and perceptual loss within a diffusion model shows promise for generating high-quality, consistent, and detailed fonts. However, the limited comparison to other state-of-the-art methods and lack of ablation studies reduce its overall impact.

Score: 6.5
**Rationale:**

The score is justified by the following factors:

*   **Clear contribution (1 point):** The DK-Font method is properly defined with its improvements over the foundational U-Net architecture.
*   **Good execution (2 points):** The quantitative results are well-presented and support the claim of better performance relative to the baseline. The visual examples also showcase the improved quality of the generated fonts.
*   **Limited novelty (1 point):** While the integration of text encoders is helpful and novel, much of the work focuses on the incorporation of existing work done within image generation to make quality improvements.
*   **Need for better evaluation (1.5 points):** The lack of comparison to SOTA methods makes it difficult to gauge the relative significance of the work and a more rigorous evaluation is needed.
*   **Area of impact (1 point):** The results can benefit researchers looking to make new font generation tools using a diffusion model.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Does the Prompt-based Large Language Model Recognize Students' Demographics and Introduce Bias in Essay Scoring?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21330v1)
- **Authors**: Kaixun Yang, Mladen Raković, Dragan Gašević, Guanliang Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used in Automated Essay Scoring (AES) due to their ability to capture semantic meaning. Traditional fine-tuning approaches required technical expertise, limiting accessibility for educators with limited technical backgrounds. However, prompt-based tools like ChatGPT have made AES more accessible, enabling educators to obtain machine-generated scores using natural-language prompts (i.e., the prompt-based paradigm). Despite advancements, prior studies have shown bias in fine-tuned LLMs, particularly against disadvantaged groups. It remains unclear whether such biases persist or are amplified in the prompt-based paradigm with cutting-edge tools. Since such biases are believed to stem from the demographic information embedded in pre-trained models (i.e., the ability of LLMs' text embeddings to predict demographic attributes), this study explores the relationship between the model's predictive power of students' demographic attributes based on their written works and its predictive bias in the scoring task in the prompt-based paradigm. Using a publicly available dataset of over 25,000 students' argumentative essays, we designed prompts to elicit demographic inferences (i.e., gender, first-language background) from GPT-4o and assessed fairness in automated scoring. Then we conducted multivariate regression analysis to explore the impact of the model's ability to predict demographics on its scoring outcomes. Our findings revealed that (i) prompt-based LLMs can somewhat infer students' demographics, particularly their first-language backgrounds, from their essays; (ii) scoring biases are more pronounced when the LLM correctly predicts students' first-language background than when it does not; and (iii) scoring error for non-native English speakers increases when the LLM correctly identifies them as non-native.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates whether prompt-based Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-40 recognize students' demographic attributes from their essays and whether this recognition introduces bias in automated essay scoring (AES). The study uses a dataset of over 25,000 argumentative essays and prompts GPT-40 to infer gender and first-language background and then score the essays.  The authors find that LLMs can infer demographics to some extent, especially first-language background. Scoring biases are present and more pronounced when the LLM correctly predicts a student's first language. Furthermore, scoring errors for non-native English speakers increase when the LLM correctly identifies them.  The study uses multivariate regression to quantify these relationships.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Strengths:*

*   **Relevance and Timeliness:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing use of LLMs in education and the ongoing concerns about algorithmic bias. Understanding the potential for bias in prompt-based LLMs is crucial.
*   **Methodological Rigor:**  The paper uses a large dataset, well-established evaluation metrics (QWK, OSA, OSD, MAED), and multivariate regression to analyze the relationships between demographic prediction and scoring bias. They are careful in their prompt design, incorporating techniques such as few-shot learning and role-assigned prompting. They also acknowledge and address potential biases stemming from imbalanced data.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are clearly defined and focused.
*   **Significant Findings:**  The findings offer important insights into the behavior of prompt-based LLMs in AES, specifically highlighting the persistence of bias related to first-language background even in the absence of fine-tuning.
*   **Reproducibility:** The inclusion of all designed prompts in the digital appendix enhances the replicability of the study.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Single LLM:** The study focuses solely on GPT-40. While it's a powerful model, the generalizability of the findings to other LLMs is uncertain.  The authors attempt to mitigate this by running each experiment five times with paraphrased prompts generated by Gemini and Claude to assess generalizability, but this is a limited form of validation.
*   **Limited Demographic Factors:**  The study primarily examines gender and first-language background. Other potentially relevant demographic factors, such as race and socioeconomic status, are not explored in depth.  The study acknowledges this limitation.
*   **Reliance on LLM's Ability to Infer:** The study focuses on the *relationship* between LLM's inference accuracy and bias, rather than directly debiasing the model or investigating why these biases occur on a fundamental level within the LLM's architecture.
*   **Correlation vs. Causation:** While the regression analysis provides evidence of a relationship, it cannot definitively establish a causal link between the LLM's ability to predict demographics and scoring bias.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in its focused investigation of bias in *prompt-based* LLMs for AES and its explicit analysis of the connection between the model's ability to predict demographics and its predictive bias.  Previous studies have largely focused on fine-tuned LLMs. This study bridges the gap, making a valuable contribution.  The findings are significant because they suggest that biases can persist even when using cutting-edge prompt-based tools, and this highlights the need for careful monitoring and mitigation strategies.  Also, the paper provides hints that bias might be related to *how* LLMs encode demographic features in linguistic patterns.

*Potential Influence:*

The study can influence the development of fairer AES systems. It can guide the design of prompts, the selection of few-shot examples, and the development of debiasing strategies for prompt-based LLMs.  It also calls for continued research into the mechanisms underlying bias in LLMs and the development of more comprehensive fairness evaluation frameworks.

**Score: 7.5**

*Rationale:* The paper makes a valuable and timely contribution by investigating bias in prompt-based LLMs for AES. The methodological rigor, clear research questions, and significant findings support a strong score.  The limitations regarding the single LLM used, the limited set of demographic factors considered, and the correlational nature of the findings prevent a higher score.  The paper builds well on existing research and offers clear direction for future work in mitigating bias in AI-driven educational tools. The identified relationships between demographic predictivity and bias also opens potential avenues for a deeper understanding of the internal representations and inductive biases in LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simple Visual Artifact Detection in Sora-Generated Videos
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21334v1)
- **Authors**: Misora Sugiyama, Hirokatsu Kataoka
- **Abstract**: The December 2024 release of OpenAI's Sora, a powerful video generation model driven by natural language prompts, highlights a growing convergence between large language models (LLMs) and video synthesis. As these multimodal systems evolve into video-enabled LLMs (VidLLMs), capable of interpreting, generating, and interacting with visual content, understanding their limitations and ensuring their safe deployment becomes essential. This study investigates visual artifacts frequently found and reported in Sora-generated videos, which can compromise quality, mislead viewers, or propagate disinformation. We propose a multi-label classification framework targeting four common artifact label types: label 1: boundary / edge defects, label 2: texture / noise issues, label 3: movement / joint anomalies, and label 4: object mismatches / disappearances. Using a dataset of 300 manually annotated frames extracted from 15 Sora-generated videos, we trained multiple 2D CNN architectures (ResNet-50, EfficientNet-B3 / B4, ViT-Base). The best-performing model trained by ResNet-50 achieved an average multi-label classification accuracy of 94.14%. This work supports the broader development of VidLLMs by contributing to (1) the creation of datasets for video quality evaluation, (2) interpretable artifact-based analysis beyond language metrics, and (3) the identification of visual risks relevant to factuality and safety.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the detection of visual artifacts in videos generated by OpenAI's Sora model. It proposes a multi-label classification framework to identify four common types of artifacts: boundary/edge defects, texture/noise issues, movement/joint anomalies, and object mismatches/disappearances. The study uses a dataset of manually annotated frames from Sora-generated videos and trains several 2D CNN architectures (ResNet-50, EfficientNet-B3/B4, ViT-Base) for multi-label classification.  The best-performing model (ResNet-50) achieves a reported average multi-label classification accuracy of 94.14%. The authors argue this work contributes to video quality evaluation, artifact-based analysis beyond language metrics, and the identification of visual risks in VidLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of detecting visual artifacts in AI-generated videos is not entirely novel. Prior work exists on detecting deepfakes and artifacts in GAN-generated images/videos. The specific application to Sora-generated content, and the defined set of artifact categories, represent incremental novelty. The approach of using standard 2D CNNs for this task lacks significant technical innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically relevant problem – ensuring the trustworthiness and quality of AI-generated content. The defined artifact categories provide a useful starting point for analyzing visual failures in Sora videos. The high accuracy achieved by a relatively simple ResNet-50 model suggests the feasibility of automated artifact detection. The dataset creation (even though small) is a valuable contribution to the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a relevant and timely problem (trustworthiness of AI-generated content).
    *   Clearly defines artifact categories relevant to Sora-generated videos.
    *   Demonstrates the feasibility of artifact detection using standard CNN architectures.
    *   Provides a manually annotated dataset, contributing to the community resources.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited dataset size (300 frames from 15 videos) may not be representative of the full range of artifacts in Sora videos.
    *   Simple methodology (standard 2D CNN architectures) lacks technical innovation.
    *   No comparison to existing deepfake detection techniques or video quality metrics.
    *   Lacks temporal analysis:  The study treats video frames as independent images, neglecting temporal dependencies that are crucial for detecting movement/joint anomalies.  This severely limits the ability to properly classify the "movement/joint anomalies" category.
    *   The claim of general applicability to VidLLMs is overstated, given the limited scope of the study (focused solely on Sora).
    *   No discussion on failure cases or limitations of the Grad-CAM analysis.

*   **Impact:** The paper could potentially influence the development of tools for evaluating the quality and trustworthiness of AI-generated videos. However, the limited scope and incremental novelty may restrict its long-term impact. Future work should focus on creating larger, more diverse datasets, exploring more sophisticated detection techniques (e.g., 3D CNNs, transformers), and integrating temporal information.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem and provides a proof-of-concept demonstration of artifact detection in Sora-generated videos. However, it suffers from limitations in dataset size, methodology, and scope. The novelty is incremental, and the impact may be limited without further improvements and extensions. A higher score could be justified with a larger dataset, more sophisticated techniques, and a more thorough evaluation.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### UniBiomed: A Universal Foundation Model for Grounded Biomedical Image Interpretation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21336v1)
- **Authors**: Linshan Wu, Yuxiang Nie, Sunan He, Jiaxin Zhuang, Hao Chen
- **Abstract**: Multi-modal interpretation of biomedical images opens up novel opportunities in biomedical image analysis. Conventional AI approaches typically rely on disjointed training, i.e., Large Language Models (LLMs) for clinical text generation and segmentation models for target extraction, which results in inflexible real-world deployment and a failure to leverage holistic biomedical information. To this end, we introduce UniBiomed, the first universal foundation model for grounded biomedical image interpretation. UniBiomed is based on a novel integration of Multi-modal Large Language Model (MLLM) and Segment Anything Model (SAM), which effectively unifies the generation of clinical texts and the segmentation of corresponding biomedical objects for grounded interpretation. In this way, UniBiomed is capable of tackling a wide range of biomedical tasks across ten diverse biomedical imaging modalities. To develop UniBiomed, we curate a large-scale dataset comprising over 27 million triplets of images, annotations, and text descriptions across ten imaging modalities. Extensive validation on 84 internal and external datasets demonstrated that UniBiomed achieves state-of-the-art performance in segmentation, disease recognition, region-aware diagnosis, visual question answering, and report generation. Moreover, unlike previous models that rely on clinical experts to pre-diagnose images and manually craft precise textual or visual prompts, UniBiomed can provide automated and end-to-end grounded interpretation for biomedical image analysis. This represents a novel paradigm shift in clinical workflows, which will significantly improve diagnostic efficiency. In summary, UniBiomed represents a novel breakthrough in biomedical AI, unlocking powerful grounded interpretation capabilities for more accurate and efficient biomedical image analysis.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces UniBiomed, a universal foundation model for grounded biomedical image interpretation. UniBiomed combines a Multi-modal Large Language Model (MLLM) with the Segment Anything Model (SAM) to simultaneously generate clinical text descriptions and segment corresponding biomedical objects across a wide range of imaging modalities. The model is trained on a large-scale dataset of over 27 million image-text-annotation triplets spanning ten biomedical imaging modalities, achieving state-of-the-art performance on segmentation, disease recognition, and report generation tasks across 84 datasets. UniBiomed aims to provide automated and end-to-end grounded interpretation for biomedical image analysis, streamlining clinical workflows and improving diagnostic efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of MLLM and SAM for a *grounded* interpretation of biomedical images. Previous approaches typically treated text generation and segmentation as disjoint tasks, or required substantial manual intervention, such as precise prompts or bounding box annotations, or specialized pre-diagnosis by clinical experts. UniBiomed's attempt to automate this in a unified framework is valuable. The curation of a very large dataset of image/text/annotation triplets is a significant contribution, addressing a key challenge in the field. However, the core architectural ideas (MLLM + SAM) are borrowed from the general computer vision domain, with the primary innovation being their adaptation and scaling for biomedical applications. The use of textual descriptions to prompt SAM may also be seen as an improvement of a new application but isn't a complete break from older prompting methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper claims to offer a novel paradigm shift by automating the grounded interpretation process. If UniBiomed achieves the promised results, it holds potential to streamline clinical workflows and reduce dependence on manual annotation and expert intervention. A key benefit is leveraging holistic biomedical information (both visual and textual). Demonstrating superior performance on a diverse set of biomedical tasks would be significant. However, the potential limitations of relying on existing clinical reports and the inherent biases within them are not thoroughly addressed. It is possible that the reliance on human-written reports can perpetuate existing limitations in diagnostic practices, even as workflow efficiency gets improved.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Large-scale dataset curation.
    *   Unified framework for text generation and segmentation.
    *   State-of-the-art performance across various biomedical tasks (if validated).
    *   Potential for streamlining clinical workflows.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Architectural novelty is incremental (adaptation of existing techniques).
    *   Potential reliance on biased training data from existing clinical reports and their limitations.
    *   Limited analysis of real-world impact in clinical settings (the paper relies on benchmark datasets).
    *   Unclear assessment of robustness against adversarial attacks.
    *   Implementation details could use additional rigor.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the claimed performance holds up in real-world deployments, UniBiomed could encourage future research into end-to-end grounded interpretation frameworks. The dataset could become a valuable resource for the community. The focus on automating diagnostic workflows is likely to resonate with clinicians and healthcare providers.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above evaluation, UniBiomed presents incremental architectural innovation applied to a practical and important problem: grounded biomedical image understanding. The creation of a large-scale dataset is a significant contribution, but the adaptation of existing architectural principles (MLLM + SAM) somewhat reduces the architectural novelty. The potential real-world impact in streamlining clinical workflows is appealing. On balance, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field, though there are a few potential limitations to consider, including biases from reliance on human-written reports and a lack of complete architectural innovation. Given these considerations, a score of 6 seems reasonable.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Nexus-Gen: A Unified Model for Image Understanding, Generation, and Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21356v1)
- **Authors**: Hong Zhang, Zhongjie Duan, Xingjun Wang, Yingda Chen, Yuze Zhao, Yu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Unified multimodal large language models (MLLMs) aim to integrate multimodal understanding and generation abilities through a single framework. Despite their versatility, existing open-source unified models exhibit performance gaps against domain-specific architectures. To bridge this gap, we present Nexus-Gen, a unified model that synergizes the language reasoning capabilities of LLMs with the image synthesis power of diffusion models. To align the embedding space of the LLM and diffusion model, we conduct a dual-phase alignment training process. (1) The autoregressive LLM learns to predict image embeddings conditioned on multimodal inputs, while (2) the vision decoder is trained to reconstruct high-fidelity images from these embeddings. During training the LLM, we identified a critical discrepancy between the autoregressive paradigm's training and inference phases, where error accumulation in continuous embedding space severely degrades generation quality. To avoid this issue, we introduce a prefilled autoregression strategy that prefills input sequence with position-embedded special tokens instead of continuous embeddings. Through dual-phase training, Nexus-Gen has developed the integrated capability to comprehensively address the image understanding, generation and editing tasks. All models, datasets, and codes are published at https://github.com/modelscope/Nexus-Gen.git to facilitate further advancements across the field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Nexus-Gen: A Unified Model for Image Understanding, Generation, and Editing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Nexus-Gen, a unified multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed for image understanding, generation, and editing. The model aims to bridge the performance gap between existing open-source unified models and domain-specific architectures. Nexus-Gen leverages the language reasoning capabilities of LLMs and the image synthesis power of diffusion models. Key features include a dual-phase alignment training process, a "prefilled autoregression" strategy to mitigate error accumulation during image embedding generation, and a unified task representation for various multimodal tasks.  The authors present qualitative results and claim improvements over existing methods in handling complex image editing instructions. They emphasize releasing all models, datasets, and code to promote further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of unifying image understanding, generation, and editing isn't entirely new. Many recent MLLMs aim for similar goals. However, the specific approach of Nexus-Gen has some interesting aspects:

    *   **Prefilled Autoregression:**  This strategy for addressing error accumulation in the continuous image embedding space appears to be the most novel contribution. While the paper claims it eliminates error accumulation, it would be beneficial to have more extensive quantitative analysis demonstrating its effectiveness over naive autoregressive methods. The provided loss curves (Figures 4 and 5) are a good start, but further analysis (e.g., generation quality comparisons) would significantly strengthen this claim.

    *   **Dual-Phase Alignment Training:**  The idea of aligning the LLM and diffusion model embedding spaces is a standard technique in MLLMs, though the specific details of Nexus-Gen's implementation could offer some novelty.

    *   **Unified Task Representation:**  While a unified task representation is crucial for multimodal models, the approach described (using  ``````````<image>```````````` tokens) is relatively straightforward.  The true value lies in the effectiveness of integrating this representation into the training process.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper depends on the degree to which Nexus-Gen outperforms existing methods. The paper relies primarily on qualitative examples which are visually appealing but do not provide a rigorous and systematic comparison. Without quantitative metrics (e.g., FID scores, CLIP scores for generation, accuracy metrics for understanding), it's difficult to assess the true impact of the work. The release of models, datasets, and code will be vital in allowing others to reproduce results and conduct further evaluations.

    *   The paper's claim that Nexus-Gen bridges the gap with domain-specific architectures requires substantial evidence. The qualitative examples show compelling results, but the lack of quantitative comparison with SOTA diffusion models significantly weakens this claim.

    *   The ability to handle complex, story-based image editing is a potentially valuable contribution. However, the paper needs to clearly demonstrate how Nexus-Gen's capabilities surpass those of conventional editing tools.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Quantitative Evaluation:** The most significant weakness is the absence of quantitative benchmarks comparing Nexus-Gen to other state-of-the-art methods. Without these comparisons, it's impossible to objectively assess the model's performance.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from ablation studies to assess the impact of different components, such as the prefilled autoregression strategy or the dual-phase alignment training.
    *   **Unclear Dataset Details:** While the paper mentions the datasets used, it lacks sufficient detail about their sizes, pre-processing steps, or potential biases.
    *   **Incremental Contributions:** Many of the components used in Nexus-Gen (pretrained LLM, diffusion model) are well-established. The incremental value hinges on the effectiveness of the proposed training strategies and architecture choices.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Approach:** The concept of creating a unified model for multiple image-related tasks is valuable and aligns with the current research direction in MLLMs.
    *   **Prefilled Autoregression:**  This is a promising technique for addressing error accumulation, and its potential impact warrants further investigation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and provides a clear overview of the proposed model and training process.
    *   **Open-Source Release:** The intention to release models, datasets, and code is commendable and crucial for reproducibility and community engagement.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the promising but unverified results, the primary novelty of the 'prefilled autoregression' method, and significant flaws, the paper's score will fall in the lower middle.
The primary negative impact is the lack of benchmarks that enable the community to quickly assess if NexusGen does in fact have an advantage over existing models.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### ShorterBetter: Guiding Reasoning Models to Find Optimal Inference Length for Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21370v1)
- **Authors**: Jingyang Yi, Jiazheng Wang
- **Abstract**: Reasoning models such as OpenAI o3 and DeepSeek-R1 have demonstrated strong performance on reasoning-intensive tasks through extended Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. While longer reasoning traces can facilitate a more thorough exploration of solution paths for complex problems, researchers have observed that these models often "overthink", leading to inefficient inference. In this paper, we introduce ShorterBetter, a simple yet effective reinforcement learning methed that enables reasoning language models to discover their own optimal CoT lengths without human intervention.
